Dear Mr. Martin:
Thanks very much for your continued help with respect to finding
out what damage was done in Jepan to glutamic acid plants.
The firm that held the patents formerly was the Suzuki Chemical
Company. It is thought that just before the Japanese surrender,
they might have changed their name to the Rising Sun Company, but
there is no definite evidence for this. I would be very pleased
if you could find out what the production of glutamic acid or
sodium glutamate is in Japan at the present time, or what is in
prospect for one year.
Thanking you again,
Yours cordially,
/8/

In reply refer to
JK
My dear Dr. Darby:
I refer again to your letter of March 29, 1946 relating to the
production of glutamic acid in Japan.
894.659/5-646
No production statictics are available in Washington for
glutamic acid or sodium glutamate, and such data would have to be
obtained from Japan. Unfortunately the pressure of work is so great
that our occupation authorities in Tokyo are unable to devote time
894.5
to investigating the status of production and the assembling of
statistical information except for commodities whose importance is
clearly established.
If you can supply me with information concerning the purpose to
which the material you request will be put, and indicate what importanse
these commodities play either in world commodity markets or in the
Japanese economy, it may be possible to obtain further statistics.
Sincerely yours,
CS/VJ

Dear Sir:
I am enclosing a copy of a letter by T. M. rewer of the Department of State
to Mr. Homer Laughlin, pottery manutacturer of East Liverpool, Ohio. Mr.
Laughlin is retired but the letter was delivered to the nomer Laughlin China
Company, who are the largest manufacturers of dinnerware in the United States.
In reading the letter (copy attached) you will note that uhe Departmemt of
State is interested in finding & Ceramic Engineer or Technician to send to
Japan at the expense of Americen tax payers, vo educate the Japs to compete
witn American manufacturers or pottery, workmen, and tax payers. I am wonder
ing if the idea of the Department of State strikes you as utterly ridiculous
as it does the writer?
During ine years 1932 to l939 tne Japs were increasingly each year, piling
dinnerware into the United States, causing us to operate only two-three and
four days a week. sy ly3y they had our prices driven down to a profitless level.
The Uffice of rice Administration adopted the years l936 to l939 as our base
period with the results tnat unis company operated at a loss in 1943-1944 and
l945, and due to our base period low prices we are still operating at a loss,
witn our manufacturing cost continually rising, due to Uffice of Price Admin-
istration not controling wnat we ouy witn tre same consistency as what we sell.
Now comes the Department of State wenting to educate the Japs in eificiency
along witn tneir low labor cost, to perpetuate tnat ruinous competition we had
to contend with 1or so many years pervious to December, l94l.
If you see eye-to-eye with us, we trust that you will use your influence or
power by virtue of your office to "squasn" this idea of tne Dept. of State.
The Japs will soon enougn, again become serious competition to tne American
Pottery industry witnout the Department of State spending money to educate
them.
Where will the money eventually come from to carry our nign cost or Government
if the Department of State feels tney are called upon to educate the Japs to
help them drive merican industry out of business?
And, I am not an isolationist!
Yours truly,
CROWN POTTERIES CO.
/s/ J. P. Wendt

DEPARTMENT OF STATE

RECEIVED MAR 21 1946
Dear Mr. Laughlin:
We are seeking your assistance in the selection of
two Ceramic Engineers to serve as advisors to the Chief of
the Economic and Scientific Section of the Military Govern-
ment and Control Activities in Japan regarding technical
problems of the Japanese Ceramic Industry.
We do not have full details regarding the duties but
we understand that these positions require highly qualified
technicians with a broad experience in the various fields
of Ceramics. The salaries will range up to 78o0 per annum.
All positions in the Military Goverrment Activities are,
of course, under the jurisdiction of the War Department.
In accordance with a cooperative arrangement, however, the
Department of State is responsible for recruiting and
recommending personnel for certain specialized positions such
as these. The minimum period for an assignment of this
nature is nine months, though it is hoped that an appointee
may remain for an indefinite period. Transportation is
provided at government expense, and living costs are
exceedingly low since the Army provides living quarters and
meals similar to those of officers at a nominal charge of
approximately 40.00 per month. Families cannot accompany
civilian appointees at present, but there is a fair prospect
that they may be able to follow later on in the summer or
fall.
We would greatly appreciate any suggestions which you
may be in a position to make regarding personnel who may
be qualified for and interested in these positions.
Very truly yours,
T. M. Brewer, Recruitment Officer
Special Recruitment Section
Division of Departmental Personel
Mr. Homer Laughlin
American Domestic Pottery Manufacturing Co.
East Liverpool, Ohio.

=== Page 21 === (confidence 0.94)
JUL 15
In reply refer to
CP 894.659/5-2846
My dear Senator Bushfield:
Further reference is made to your letter of
May 28, 1946 quoting statements by one of your consti-
tuents regarding Singer sewing machines and their role
in United States - South American trade.
8
6
After making a number of informal inquiries, I have
4.6
the following comments to make on the statements referred
to. The statements will be discussed in the order of
their appearance in the letter.
6
(1) "...great quantities of Singer sewing machine
parts prior to the war were made in Japan, and assembled
5
in America."
chro
It is true that Japan was manufacturing so-called
3S
Singer machine parts as well as the finished produet.
17
This was done by obtaining old Singer models and copying
9
them. Apparently, Japanese patent and trade-mark regu-
lations permitted this practice and there was no legal
recourse against it available to interested parties in
this country. I cen find no evidence, however, that
the Japanese-manufactured parts were assembled anywhere
in the United States. It is certain that they were not
assembled here and marketed as Singer machines. I am
informed, moreover, that the Singer Sewing Machine
Company has no factory or assembly plant in Japan.
(2) "That the bulk of Singer sewing machines do-
livered to parts of the world other than Ameriea were
made in Japan."

As stated above, the Japanese machines were modeled
after Singer machines. In many pleces, where so allewed,
they were marketed as Singer machines. It would require
extensive statistieal research to determine whether
these machines made in Japan were sold in greater quan-
tities in the area specified than the product of the
Singer Sewing Machine Company. It may be eorreet that
the so-called Singers of Japanese origin dominated the
market in the Far Eastern area. It is known that this
is not true with regard to many European markets and
also many markets in the other parts of the world.
(3) "Also that the attachments to sewing machines
used in America were made in Japan."
No factual basis can be found for this statement.
(4) "I believe you will find that the machines
that went to South America were paid for with South
American grain and meat whioh were brought into the
United States with little or no tariff and served to
depress the price of the foodstuffs produced by the
American farmer, and consequently made less jobs for
Amoricans."
Presumably the foregoing reference is to machines
assembled in the United States from Japanese-produced
parts, and hence the entire statement is based upon a
false premise. Even if the reference is to maehines
completely manufactured in the United States by the
Singer Sewing Machine Company, the statement is none-
theless entirely without foundation.
Normally this country is wholly self-sufficient in
grains. Except for some imports of corn during several
years of oritical shortage, grain imports into the United
States from South America, or elsewhere, have been vir-
tually nil. In conformity d th recommendations of the
International Emergency Food Board this country has been
exporting grains to meet the essential requirements of
various nations, both during and since the war.
With regard to meat imports, it should be observed
that regulations issued by the United States Department
of Agrioulture pursuant to Section 306(a) of the Tariff
Act of 1930 prohibit the importation of all cattle,
sheep, swine, and fresh, chilled or frosen beef, veal,
mutton

=== Page 23 === (confidence 0.97)
- 3-
wutton, lamb and pork from countries in whieh it has
been found that rinderpest or foot-and-mouth disease
exists. This prohibition has been applied to Argentima
as well as other South Amerioan countries. The resuls
is that the only meat 1mports of consequenee from South
Americs have been canned or processed meats and, to a
considerably less extent, some poultry. Moreover, what
imports there have been of canned meats sinee the be-
ginning of the war have gone entirely to the Armed
Forces of the United States. In brief, the only meat
imports of significant volume into the United States
from South America were pre-war imports of proeessed
meats, the value of which was much less than that of
many other commodities provided by the same region.
As regards the tariff on meat and meat produots, it
is generally quite high. The same situation prevails
with respect to the most important grains.
The volume of grain and meat imports has been so
insignificant in comparison with domestie production of
these items that it is inoonceivable that imports have
at any time served to depress the price of the domestie
foodstuffs.

has been taken by this office.

=== Page 28 === (confidence 0.99)
CONFIDENTIAL
DEPARTMENT OF THE ARMY
OFFICE OF THE UNDER SECRETARY
WASHINGTON, D. C.
.5- MAR 1948
Honorable Charles E. Saltzman
Assistant Secretary of State
Dear Mr. Saltzman:
This is in answer to your communication dated 2 February 1948
introduced by mention of reported proposals for shipments of alumina
into Japan for processing there. As you doubtless know, reprèsenta-
tives of both the Reynolds Company and the Permanente Corporation,
subsequent to conferences with representatives of the Departments of
State and Army, went to Japan to discuss with SCAP the possibility
of reducing alumina in Japanese plants. No proposals have yet been
received from SCAP as a result of these negotiations. In fact, SCAP
894.
has made no proposals for importing alumina into Japan in order to
permit the export of aluminum ingot from Japan, or other proposals
for aluminum production for export which would necessarily require
approval by the United States Government.
As you know, a representative of the Permanente Corporation has
approached officials of this Department as well as the Department of
State concerning prospects for obtaining aluminum, aluminum products
and steel products from Japan. The Permanente representative sugges-
/3-548
ted to Department of the Army representatives that his organization
and affiliates might be willing to supply raw materials and other im-
ports into Japan in order to accomplish the necessary production to
provide the products in which they were interested. It was suggested
to the Permanente representative that no policydifficulty appeared
to be involved in connection with their interest in obtaining steel
products. In connection with their interest in aluminum and aluminum
products, it was pointed out that negotiations in the FEC were some-
what delicate concerning approval for retention ofaluminum producing
capacity in Japan. It was suggested that if they would emphasize their
interest in aluminum products, the matter might be handled more favor-
ably than would an interest in aluminum ingots. In any event, it was
suggested to them that any contracts which they might develop for ob-
taining either aluminum or aluminum products should guarantee that un-
der no circumstances would the operation of the contract delay to any
significant degree reparations removals of aluminum producing capacity
once a policy determination is crystallized respecting the amount of
aluminum capacity which Japan mayretain and the capacity which must
be made available for reparation removals. The position expressed
above to the Permanente representative was discussed with Messrs. Dean
Bowman and Burr Smith of E of your Department who indicated general
agreement with the position as stated.

NATURAL RESOURCES SECTION
REPORT NUMBER 55
10 September 1946
FERTILIZERS IN JAPAN
(A Preliminary Report)
SUMMARY
i. The soils of Japan are naturally infertile. Centuries of
intensive cropping have decreased the amount of plant food elements in
the soil to a point where it is necessary to apoly fertilizers to obtain
high crop yields. Small apolications of commercial fertilizers during
the later years of the war (1943-1945) have decreased still further the
already low plant food reserves in the soil.
2. Agricultural practices in Japan favor continued deterioration of
the soils. Among these practices are (1) multiple cropping of much of
the land; (2) lack of a systematized crop rotation plan which includes a
legume as one of the crops grown; and (3) a livestock economy which
permits only small numbers of livestock on the farms with a consequent
minimum production of barayard manures.
3. The consumption of all kinds of commercial fertilizers declined
from a maximum average annual consumption of 4,136,000 metric tons 1/ for
the 1936-1940 period to 471,000 metric tons, or about 11.4 percent, in
1945.
4. Maximum consumption of commercial fertilizers in Japan occurred
during 1936-1940. Average annual consumption for this period by kinds of
commercial fertilizers, including both organic and inorganic forms, in
metric tons was nitrogenous, 2,082,000; phosphatic, 1,834,000; and
potassic, 220,000. During 1945 the estimated low consumption of com-
mercial fertilizers in metric tons was nitrogenous, 423,000; phosphatic,
44,000; and potassic, 4,000.
5. Commercial fertilizer stocks are at an all-time low in Japan.
Only 63,800 metric tons were consumed during Mey 1946 as against the
average monthly consumption of 345,000 metric tons during 1936-1940.
6. The supply of commercial fertilizers from indigenous production
facilities was impeded during the war (194l-1945) by scarcity of raw
materials, damage to factories by American bombing raids, land and water
transportation difficulties, and scarcity of labor. All of these fectors
1/ Fertilizer equivalent basis (Table 51)
7

=== Page 42 === (confidence 0.99)
have been carried over into l946, except probably for labor, which is
now reported to be abundant.
7. Farm manures occupy an important position in the soil fertility
program of Japan. The total consumption of all kinds of farm manures on
a fertilizer equivalent basis has increased from a low of 2,548,000
metric tons in 1926 to a high of 4,723,000 metric tons in 1945.
8. Natural deposits of either phosphatic or potassic materials of
real commercial value do not exist in Japan. Plent facilities for the
processing of phosphatic raw materials into superphosphate, and for the
production of nitrogenous fertilizers are adequate for meeting these
fertilizer needs of Japan. Facilities for processing potassic raw
materials into potassic fertilizers are practically noneristent in Japan.
9. An expected increase in the population of Japan because of
normal population increases and the repatriation of Japanese nationals
to the homeland will necessitate either additional food production or.
food importation.
10. The estimated reclamation of 347,l00 hectares of land in 1946
by the Imperial Japanese Government will require that fertilizers be
added to this reclaimed land to insure even a reasonable production of
crops.
11. Field experiments.with fertilizers in Japan show conclusively
the beneficial effects of adequate fertilization on crop yields.
12. It is believed that for marimized production of crops in l946
a minimum amount of commercial fertilizers consisting of 1,800,000 metric
tons of nitrogenous fertilizers (20 percent nitrogen (N) content);
1,387,500 metric. tons of phosphatic fertilizers (16 percent phosphoric
acid (P205) content); and 150.000 metric tons of potassic fertilizers
(50 percent potassium oxide (Kz0) content) should be produced in Japan
or imported from other sources.
13. If the amounts of 2,000,000 metric tons of nitrogenous,
1,500,000 metric tons of phosphatic, and 250,000 metric tons of potassic
commercial fertilizers become available, no apparent reason exists for
not distributing these quantities of fertilizers to the farmers of
Japan.
14. Priority of importation of raw materials or processed ferti-
lizer products into Japan should be (1) phosphatic, (2) nitrogenous,
and (3) potassic fertilizers. . Priority of production of fertilizers in
Japan should be (1) nitrogenous and phosphatic, and (2) potassic
fertilizers.
8

=== Page 43 === (confidence 0.99)
NATURAL RESOURCES SECTION
REPORT NUMBER 55
10 September 1946
FERTILIZEPS IN JAPAN 2/
(A Preliminary Report)
A. Introduction
1. Closely allied with thc critical food situation / expeoted
in Japan during 1946 will be the availability of adequate amounts of
fertilizers for agricultural crops. If Japan is to produce for domestic
consumption in 1946 the estinated food requirement of 27,633,000 metric
tons of foodatuffs 4/, it is esaantial that present soil fertility levele
be maintained by application of ell available kinds of fertilizers, both
commercial fertilizers and farm manurcs
2. It is well kmown that Japan makes efficient use of all
available indigenous farn-produced fertilizer materials. Organic wastes
such as leaves, grasses, potato vines, rice and wheat straw, and similar
materials are used as fertilizers by adding them to the soil as green
manures or. by first conposting them to make the plant nutrients which
they contain in a form more readily available to plants and then applying
the composted organic wastes to the soil. Night soil (human excrement)
also plays an important part in the fertilizer economy of Japan.
Although Japan apparently does make maximum use of farm manures 5/, it
2/ This report was prepared by Major 3 L W Swanson, Head of the Soils
and Fertilizer Branch, Agriculture Division, on military leave from
the Agronomy Department, University of New Hampshire, and Mr, T
Tanada, Scientific Consultant, Agriculture Division, on leave from
the Agricultural Experiment Station, University of Hawaii. The data
presented in the tables on fertilizers also include Okinawa because
this island before the Pacific War (194l-1945), was administratively
a part of Japan. The amounts,' in any case, for Okinawa are less than
one percent of the total amounte. None of the statistics included in
this report for the year 1946 pertain to Okinawa. Japan now consists

will be necessary to supplement them with commercial fertilizers in i946 . .

3. Because of their natural composition and long-continued
intensive cultivation, the soils of Japan are infertile. They are
especially deficient in the plant nutrients nitrogen, phosphorus, and
potash. Removal of plant nutrient elements from the soil .by continuous
cropping of tha land for over 2,000 years in many areas of Japan has
decresed plant-food reserves in the soil. Small applications of commer-
cial fertilizers to the soil during the war period 1941-1945 (Tables 13-
15) have further depleted the plant-food stocks in the soil.
4. Information furnished by the Ministry of Agriculture and
Forestry indicates that for several years prior to the outbreak of World
War II the soils of Japan were heavily fertilized in comparison to other
caintries. Comparison of the average amounts of the commercial plant
foods consumed by the major agricultural countries of the world during
1935-1937, in terms of kilograms per hectare of total arable land, shows
that Japan was third in the total amount of nitrogen (N, 41.9 kg). fifth
in phosphoric acid (P205, 38.6 kg), and fifth in the total amount of
potash (K20, 16.9 kg) consumed 6/. When world consumption of the amount
of commercial fertilizers used by each country is considered, Japan
ranked third for nitrogenous fertilizers, and sixth for phoaphatic and
potassic fertilizers. Yet Japan ranked nineteenth in the world distri-
bution of arable lande.
5. The production of rice, the most important crop in Japan
(Figures 1 and 2), declined from an average annual production of
9,883,238 metric tons for 1936-1940 to 8,594,956 metric tons for 1941-
1945. a decrease of 13 percent. For 1945 the production of rice was
only 6,445,100 metric tons 7/. Official data of the Ministry of
Agriculture and Forestry show that 9.6 percent of the 29 percent
reduction in rice yields for 1945 was due to causes other than unfavor-
able weather, typhoons, and floods, which were particularly devastating
to the rice crop in 1945. Fractically all of this 9.6 percent reduction
in crop yield can be attributed to the lack of sufficient quantities
of fertilizers for application to growing crops.

the shortage of raw materials from overseas, and the bomb damage to many

fertilizer recommendations for each of the major crops grown in Japan.
In the case of the paddy rice plant, the amounts of.nitrogen () recom-

B. Long-time Field Experiments with Fertilizers
1. Evidence of fertilizer needs and amounts required to main-
tain reasonable levels of crop production in Japan can be obtained from
field experimentel data with fertilizers 10/.
2. Experiments on the effects of fertilizers on the yield of
paddy rice have been conducted at many of the prefectural agricultural
experiment stations in Japan. Experimental results from 10 stations
distributed over Japan show that on the average paddy rice yields for
plots receiving no fertilizers were only 54 percent of the yields from
plots receiving complete fertilization (nitrogenous, phosphatic, and
potassic); 59 percent when nitrogenous fertilizers were omitted; 94
percent when phosphatic fertilizers were not.applied, and 92 percent
when potassic fertilizers were not added as one of the plant food
elements. For upland rice, the effects of removal of nitrogenous
10/ Information from Ministry of Agriculture and Forestry
12

=== Page 47 === (confidence 0.99)
fertilizers were about the same as for paddy rice. Mimination of
phosphatic and potassic fertilisers, however, resulted in average yields
of 85 and 80 percent, respectively, for upland rice and 94 and 92 percent,
respectively, for paddy rice in comparison to yields obtained from com-
plete fertilisation.
3. Experiments conducted continously for 16 years on the same
plots at the Imperial Agricultural Experiment Station, Konosu, Saitama
Prefecture, show that no fertilizer was applied, paddy rice yields were
about 60 percent of those receiving complete fertilization. The data also
show that when organic fertilizers were used as sources of nitrogen, the
yields of paddy rice were about the same as when inorganic sources of
nitrogen were used.
4. Potash was found to be the plant food element most needed in
growing sweetpotatoes in field experiments conducted at the Chiba Prefec-
tural Agricultural Experiment Station.
5. Fertilizer experiments with barley and wheat have been
conducted at several prefectural agricultural experiment stations. The
plant food elements nitrogen, phosphorus, and potash were involved in
various fertilizer combinations. In general, the yields of barley and
wheat showed the greatest decrease when nitrogen was omitted. The average
percentage yields for wheat, covered barley, and naked barley with nitro-
gen omitted were 30, 46, and 38 percent, respectively, of the yields from
complete fertilization. When phosphate was omitted as a fertilizer in
this experiment, the average yields in comparison to complete fertili-
zation were wheat, 62 perçent; covered barley, 60 percent; and naked
barley, 77 percent. When potash was eliminated, the average yields in
comparison to complete fertilization were wheat, 53 percent; covered
barley, 82 percent; and naked barley, 84 percent.
6. wenty-year continuous field experiments with wheat at the
Imperial Agricultural Experiment Station, Konosu, Saitama Prefecture,
show that when no fertilizers were applied, wheat yields were only 32
percent of those from the completely fertilized plots. Of three plots
that received equivalent amounts of nitrogen but in different forms, the
plot receiving nitrogen in the form of ammoniun sulphate had the highest
average yield for a 2O-year period. The plot that received one-half of its
its nitrogen in the inorganic form (ammonium sulphate) and the other half
in the organic form (soybean cake and stable manure) yielded about the
same as the inorganic-nitrogen plot for the 20-year period. The plot
which had all of its nitrogen applied in the organic form (soybean cake
and stable manure) yielded 84 percent as much wheat as the inorganic-
nitrogen plot.

2. Commercial Fertilizers
a. Production of inorganic commercial nitrogenous fertilizers
in Japan reached a maximum in 194l when 1,553,000 metric tons were manu-
factured. For organic commbrcial nitrogenous fertilizers the highest
production, 281,000 metric tons, was attained in 1939 (Table 3). From
1926 to 1945, the average annual production of commercial nitrogenous
fertilizers was greatest during 1936-1940, when an average of 1,813,000
metric tons was maintained (Table 22). Production of the inorganic forms
declined rapidly after 1941 to only 323,000 metric tons in 1945; for the
orgenic forms the production decreased to 12,000 metric tons.
b. Maximum production for inorganic commercial phosphatic
fertilizers in Japan was reached in 1940 when 1,796,000 metric tons were
processed; the highest production of organic forms, 224,000 metric tons.
was attained in 1936 (Table 4). The best five-year production period for
both inorganic and organic forms was from 1936 to 1940 when tne yearly
average was 1,936,000 metric tons (Table 22).

3. Farm Manures
a. Farm manures include organic products produced on the
farm, night soil, and other organic products obtained from urban areas
which can be used as fertilizers. Of all the fertilizers used in Japan,
the production of farm manures has fluctuated less than that of any other
fertilizer consumed. From 1926 to 1945, the making of compost by the
farmers has steadily increased while the production of green manures and
night soil has remained practically constant. The production of other
manures such as poultry manure and ashes has increased fron about
8,000,000 metric tons in 1926 to approximately 10,000,000 metric tons in
1945 (Table 6). The maximum production period for farm manures was
1941-1945 (Tab1e 22).

2. Commercial Fertilizers
a. At the beginning of the 1926-1945 period, more organic
than inorganic commercial nitrogenous fertilizers were imported. From
1936 to 1940, however, inorganic imports were greater (Tables 10, 23, 28,
43-47). These were also the years of maximum fertilizer importation.
Imports declined sharply after 1940. Only about 40,000 metric tons of
inorganic and 3,000 metric tons of organic commercial nitrogenous ferti-
lizers were brought into the country in 1945.
b. Only small quantities of processed phosphatic fertilizers
have ever been imported into Japan, the organic forms averaging from six
to eight times more than the inorganic forms from 1926 to 1945 (Tables
11, 23, 43-47). No inorganic commercial phosphatic fertilizers were
delivered in 1944 or 1945, but about 1,000 metric tons of organic com-
mercial phosphatic fertilizers were brought in during 1945.
c. Practically all of the commercial potassic fertilizers
used in Japan have been imported from Germeny, the United States, France;
and Spain. Japan has neither adequate facilities nor the necessary raw
materials for manufacturing potassic fertilizers. Imports of inorgaric
commercial potassic fertilizers increased from an annual average of
65,000 metric tons fron 1926 to 1930 to 189,000 metric tons during 1936-
1940. From 1941 to 1945 an annual average of only 11,000 metric tons
was imported. After 194l, no inorganic commercial fertilizers were
received (Tables 12, 23, 46-48). Foreign deliveries of organic forms
decreased steadily from a 35,000 metric ton yearly average during 1926 to
1930 to only a 9.000 metric ton average for 1941 to 1945.

E. Consumption of Fertilizers
7. Commercial Fertilizers
a. Consumption of commercial fertilizers in Japan from
1926 to l945, except for potassic fertilizers, was primarily from indige-
nous production. Commercial fertilizers are either sold as such or mixed
with other kinds of fertilizers (prganic and inorganic) and then sold to
the farmers (Table 37). Farm manures consumed in Japan are equally as
importent as commercial fertilizers as sources of plant nutrients (Tables
6, 9, 13–18, 24, 26–29).
b. In the early years between 1926 end 1945, more organic
than inorganic forms of commercial nitrogenous fertilizers were consumed,
but in the maximum consumption period from 1936 to 1940, the ratio was
about four to one in favor of the inorgenic. Consumption of inor anic
commercial fertilizers in Japan hes ranged fror e maximum of 1,802,000
metric tone (1937) to only about 404,000 metric tons (1945). The
decrease in the use of organic commercial nitrogenous fertilizers was
proportionally greater. Only about 19,000 metric tons were applied to
the land in 1945, while 1n 1938 approximately 539,000 metric tons were
used (Table 13).

for 1945. Since no data for the area multiple cropped during 1941-
1945 are awailable, the data for 193l-1935 vere selected for it
probably is more representative then that available for other periods.

=== Page 57 === (confidence 0.99)
d. The amounts of potassic fertilizers obtained from farm
manures steadily increased until 1941-1945 when an average annual amount
of 797,000 metric tons were used (Table 24). Throughout the 1926-1945
period the largest amounts.of potash were secured from compost and barn-
yard manure (Table 18). The amounts of potash (K2O) contained in appli-
cations of farm manures from 1936 to 1945, by five-year periods, increased
from an initial annual average of 39.1 kilograms per hectare of cultivated
land to 68.2 kilograms (Table 32).
e. Although night soil is an important fertilizer used in
the growing of crops in Japan, maximum use is now being curtailed because
of a serious shortage of motor trucks and carts necessary for its
collection and transportation from the cities to the farms.. Collection
of night soil in the large cities has broken down to the point where, in
some instances, it is being dumped into rivers and the sea.
F. Fertilizer Exports
1. Exports of commercial fertilizers from Japan, in comparison
to the amounts produced, imported, and consumed, were mall during 1926
to 1945. Much of the commercial fertilizers exported were shipped to
Korea 19/:
2. From the standpoint of tonnage, ammonium sulphate has been
the most important nitrogenous fertiliser exported from Japan annually
from 1936 to 1945; superphophate has been the principal pho sphatic
fertilizer exported. Shipments of potassic fertilizers have been compara-
tively amall, less than 2,000 metric tons per year (Tables 19. 21, 25).
Exports of all fertilizers declined markedly from 194l to 1945, the amount
being negligible after 1943.
3. The maximm export year for organic and inorganic commercial
nitrogenous fertilizers was 1936 with 299,000 metric tons; for phosphatic
fertilizers it was 1940 with 268,000 metric tons: and for potassic ferti-
lizers 1937 was the best year with 4,500 metric tons (Tables 19-21).
G. Prices and DistAbution of Commercial Fertilizers
1. Production, prices, end distribution of commercial ferti-
lizers from manufacturer to consumer have always been rigidly controlled
by legislation enacted by the Japanese Government. This legislation was
to have insured adequate fertilization of crops for maximum food pro-
duction on the limited arable lands of the country. Prices to farmers
have been fixed at low levels to encourage liberal applications of ferti-
lizers to growing crops. To encourage commerical fertiliser production,
government subsidies have been made to the price pool organizations;
these subsidies supplemented payments to the manufacturers for their

products. Estimates of prices paid by the farmer in 1945 for the
principal commercial fertilizers per metric ton were given by the
Ministry of Agriculture and Forestry as ammonium sulphate, I 105.07:
calcium cyanam1de. * 73.87; amd superphosphate, ? 58.65. The prices
paid to the manufacturers were ammonium sulphate, ¥ 459.89; calcium
cyanamide, 3 292.90; and superphosphate,  275.32. Government subsidies
for fertilisers were more than four times the price the farmer paid for
them.
2. According to the Ministry of Agriculture and Forestry, no
subsidies will be paid to the manufacturers in 1946; the increase in
cost will be borne entirely by the farmers. It is estimated by the.
Ministry that the cost of fertilizers per koku of rice to the farmers
will be ¥ 8.3l, which they consider reasonable in view of the present
selling price of rice.
3. Industrialists claim that it costs $ 1,300 to produce one

that $ 1,Oll.48 is the actual cost of production, including a five-
percent dividend return to the fertilizer company. Table 40 lists the
official prices of the Ministry of Agriculture and Forestry effective
1 January 1946 for the important commerciel fertilizers used in Japan,
and also the new prices effective 1 April l946. No study of the effect
of prices on production, distribution, and use has been made.
4. Allotments of fertilizers to prefectures are made by the
Ministry of Agriculture and Forestry twice annually (August-December
and January-July) after-plans for planting have been submitted. The
various sections in the Ministry which determine the allotments are
shown in Figure 7. The agricultural essociations in each prefecture
then decide upon allotments of fertilizers to be made to the agricultural
associations of cities, towns, and villages under their jurisdiction.
These associations determine individual allotments to the fermers. The
channels of distribution of commercial fertilizers in Japan Proper are

. c. Using the Ministry of Agriculture and Forestry figures
that one ton of phosphate rock (33 percent P205 content) will produce
1.85 ton of superphosphate (16 percent P205 content), about 62,900 metric
tons of superphosphate could be produced in Japan in 1945 from the 34,000
metric tons of phosphate rock imported. The Ministry data indicate that
only 8,000 metric tons were produced (Teble 4). This situation was
probably caused by insufficient amounts of sulphuric acid, demage to war
factories by American bombing raids, transportation difficulties, labor
shortages, and other factors attendant to wartime conaitions. Even if
the remaining amount of phosphate rock is available for conversion into
superphosphate, the amount is too small to alleviate the critical need
for phosphatic fertilizers in Japan during 1946. Only small amounts of
phosphate rock (32.900 metric tons) have been imported from.1 January to
1 May 1946.
d. During the latter months of 1945, the effects of wer
conditions materially hindered the production of fertilizers and their
effects are being carried over into 1946. Materials for commercial
or anic fertilizers were reduced not only by these causes, but also by
demands on these materials for supplementing food shortages.
e. Information furnished by the Ministry of Agriculture and
Forestry shows the fertilizer situation for Japen in 1946 to be critical,
having deteriorated gradually during the war years from 194l to 1945.
The total commercial fertilizer consumption in 1945 was only about 11.4
percent of the average consumption during 1936-1940.
2. Estimated Commercial Fertilizer Requirements for 1946
a. Increese in the arable area of Japan in 1946 by reclaim-
ing an estimated area of 347;095 hectares of land will require additional
amounts of fertilizers for application to growing crops 20/. Multiple
croppin:, or the growing of more than one crop each year, on some of this
reclaimed land in southern Japan will increase further the need for
fertilizers. The area planned to be planted to crops (including multiple
cropping) is 784,000 hectares lore than was planted in 1939, the peak
year for total cropped area in Japan. Since 347,095 hectares are beinE
planned to be reclaimed in l946, and the area to be planted to green
manure crops 1s about twice the average erea (Tables 34 and 38), the
increase in size of cropped area appears to be reasonable. It is
believed, however, that the estimates for both land reclamation work and
≥0/ Estimate of the Lend Development Bureau, Ministry of Agriculture and
Forestry, Imperisl Japanese Government

=== Page 62 === (confidence 0.99)
green-manure crops are high. It is known that actual progress on land
reclamation is being retarded because of a lack of equipment and food
and housing shortages for workers. Present shortages of green-mamure
seeds will limit the area planted to these crops.
b. According to estimates furnished by the Ministry of
Agriculture and Forestry, the total requirements for l946 for commercial
fertilizers are about 2,000,000 metric tons of ammonium sulphate, or the
equivalent in other commercial nitrogenous fertilizers; 1,500,000 metric
tons of superphosphate, or the equivalent in other commercial phosphatic
fertilizers; and about 250,000 metric tons of potassium sulphate, or the
equivalent in other commercial potassic fertilizers. These requirements
are broken down by major crops, by districts, and by quarter-year in
Tables 34, 35, and 36, respectively.
c. For commercial nitrogenous fertilisers (organic and
inorganic) the maximum amount (2,000,000 metric tons) requested will be
82,000 metric tons less than was used annually during the maximum con-
sumption period, 1936-1940, and 312,000 metric tons less than the amount
'used during the maximum consumption year 1938 (Table 13). It is believed
that a minimum amount of 1,800,000 metric tons of nitrogenous fertilizers
should be made available for agricultural purposes for the year 1946.
Rice, a heavy annual user of nitrogen (Tables 34 and 35) is the basic
food for the Japanese people. Enough fertilizer should be provided to
insure its marimum production.
d. For phosphates, it is believed that a minimum amount of
750,000 metric tons of high-grade phosphate rock averaging 33 percent
phosphoric acid (P205) should be imported into Japan during 1946. This
amount of phosphate rock will produce about 1, 387,500 metric tons of
superphosphate (Table 58). The average annual .consumption of commercial
phosphatic fertilizers (organic and inorganic) for the maximum con-
sumption period, 1936-1940, was 1,834,000 metric tons (Table 24); in
1937, the peak consumption year, about 2,132,000 metric tons were applied
to crops in Japan (Table 14). Soils derived from volcanic ash in Japan
are naturally phosphate deficient. 21/ 22/. To obtain reasonable yields
from crops grown on these soils, it is necessary that phosphatic ferti-
lizers be used. In 1945, according to Ministry of Agriculture and
Forestry data, only 2.4 percent of the 1936-1940 annual average amount
of commercial phosphatic fertilizers were applied to the soils of Japan.
e. It is believed that a minimum of 150,000 metric tons of
commercial potassic fertilizers will be required for meeting the potassic
fertilizer requirements of Japan for 1946. This will be about 70,000
metric tons less than the average annual consumption for the period,

1936-1940, and about 151,000 metric tons less than the greatest amount
ever used in Japan (1937) for crop production purposes (Tables 15 and 24).
Of the three plant foods, N, P, and K, production of crops will be least
affected by a deficiency of potassium, except for both white and sweet-

larger amounts probably caniot be obtained in l946 because of the present
relatively poor operating condition of the fertilizer plant production
facilities in Japan, the tight shipping conditions which now exist for
importing materials, and the world-wide shortage of fertilizers.
g. In estimating the total nitrogenous, phosphatic, and
potassic commercial fertilizer requirements for l946, it is assumed that
only small amounts of organic commercial fertilizers will be available
for application to crops. Organic fertilizers such as fish and soybean
meal are edible. Because of the world food shortage, probably only small
amounts will be used for fertilizing purposes. It is believed, therefore,
that practically all of the commercial fertilizers used in Japan during
1946 will be those produced from inorganic materials.
h. Although smaller amounts of commercial fertilizers will
be available during 1946, part of the short supply will be supplemented
by increased amounts of farm manures expected to be available. From the
1956-1940 to the 1941-1945 periods, average annual production of farm
manures increased in the following amounts: 415,000 metric tons or nitro-
genous materials; 259,000 metric tons of phosphatic materials; and 157,000
metric tons of potassic materials (Table 22).
I. Discussion and Conclusions
1. Fertilizer Statistics for the 20-year period, 1926-1945
a. Large quantities of phosphate rock for superphosphate
manufacture, ammonium sulphate, potassium salts, and organic fertilizers
were imported into Japan during 1934-1940; imports of these materials
declined rapidly after 1940 to negligible amounts in 1945. Because of
the blockade and other losses in shipping, the decline in imports of
these materials had greatly reduced the quantities of fertilizers avail-
able for consumption in 1944. The situation was worse in 1945. Further-
more, damage to fertilizer manufacturing plants by American bombing raids
greatly hindered production, further reducing available supplies of com-
mercial fertilizers.
29

=== Page 64 === (confidence 0.99)
b. The ratio of consumption of inorganic commercial ferti-
lizers to organic fertilizers has varied considerably since l926. During
the maximum consumption year of 1937, 795,000 metric tons of organic and
3,910,000 metric tons of inorganic commercial fertilizers were used as
fertilizer material for the growing of crops in Japan. Calculated on the
fertilizer equivalent basis, consumption of organic commercial fertilizers
was about 40.0 percent of that of all commercial fertilizers consumed in
1926; in 1937 it was approximately 17.0 percent, and in 1945 the uso of
organic commercial fertilizers as e source of plant food for crops in
Japan had dropped to about 6.6 percent (Tables 13-15, 29).
c. The production of commercial inorganic fertilizers
increased with appearance of new facilities for their production, while
soybean cake, other vegetable cake, and fish products were more profit-
ably converted to food. As the need for food increased each year,
smaller amounts of organic materials were available for conversion into
organic fertilizers.
d. The years of high exports of organic. and inorganic
nitrogenous, phoaphatic, and potassic commercial fertilizers were also
years of high consumption (Tables 13-15, 19-21, 26-28).
e. Consumption (equivalent to production) of farm namures
was greatest in 1944 and 1945. It is estimated that 2,510,000 metric
tons of nitrogenous fertilizer equivalents, 1,353,000 metric tons of
phosphatic fertilizer equivalents, end £60,000 metric tons of potassic
fertilizer equivalents, or a total of 4,723,000 metric tons or ferti-
lizers, were obtained from farm manures during each of these years
(Tables 16-18, 26-28).
f. Consumption of all kinds of fertilizers, commercial
and farm manures, reached its peak during the year 1937 when 8,193,000
metric tons were used in fertilizing. the crops grown in Japan during
that year. This was also the yeer of greatest consumption of all kinds
of commercial fertilizers (4,705,000 metric tons), but not for farm
manures. About 57.4 percent of the fertilizers consumed in 1937 were
commercial fertilizers.
g. Consumption of all kinds of fertilizers dropped about
3,000,000 metric tons, or 36.6 percent, from 1937 to 1945. All of this
decrease can be attributed to the decline in the use of commercial
fertilizers, which decreased about 4,234,000 metric tons or 90.0
percent, while the consumption of farm manures increased about 1,235,000
metric tons or 35.4 percent (Table 29). Decline in the use of com-
mercial fertilizers is due mainly to the decline in indigenous pro-
duction and imports of commercial fertilizers after 1937; this decline
in production and imports, was accentuated after 194l, until in 1945 the
amounts in each case were ne ligible.
30

=== Page 65 === (confidence 0.99)
2. Iinportance of Farm Manures in the Soil Fertility Program
a. Farm manures have always been important in the soil
fertility program of Japan. About 50 percent of the plant-food nitrogen,
over 35 percent of the plant-food phosphorus, and over 74 percent of the
plant-food potash, have always been obtained from farm manures (Table 30).
These amounts were increased to about 66 percent for nitrogen, 64 percent
for phosphorus, and 97 percent for potash during the period,.194l-1945,
when the role of farm manures in the fertility program became of increased
importance because of the decrease in the use of commercial fertilizer's
furing that period. Officials of the Ministry of Agriculture and Forestry
have indicated that farm manures would continue to play an important role
in the manurial program of Japen in 1946 (Table 9).
b. Night soil, widely
applied to the soils of Japan in the
past, will continue to be used, and
is expected to contribute proportion-
ately as much in the 1946 fertilizer
program (Figure 9). During the
maximum consumption year for com-
mercial fertilizers (1937), night
soil contributed 9.4 percent of the
total amount of nitrogen contained
in commercial fertilizers and farm
manures applied that yeer to the
soils of Japan, 3.1 percent of all
the phosphc us, and 7.6 percent of
all the potash, or a combined total
of 6.8 percent (Table 31). The
Figure 9. - Transporting night
importance of night soil increesed
soil from the city to the farm
with the decrease in consumption of
November 1945
commercial fertilizers. In 1945
about 1l.8 percent of all plant
foods applied to growing crops were obtained from night soil.
c. Because of a critical lack of feed the decrease in total
numbers of livestock from 1944 to 1945 1s 1ikely to continue in 1946 23/.
which will mean a decrease in the production of barnyard manures. The
Ministry, however, has estimated no reduction in the amount of barnyard
manures for 1946. .In comparison to other countries, Japan has mall
numbers of livestock on each farm. In l940, for example, Japan had an
average of 0.209 horses, 0.37 cattle, 0.02 sheep. 0.12 hogs, and 8.21
chickens on each farm in comparison to the 2.4 horses and mules, 11.2
cattle, 8.6 sheep, 10.0 hogs, and 93.8 chickens per farm in the United
States 24/. Although the scarcity of livestock indicates that increased
23
Report No 15, Natural Resources Section, pp 1-27, 21 January 1946
24

=== Page 66 === (confidence 0.99)
production of barnyard manures
cannot be expected in 1946, the
farmers of Japan will probably
make as near maximum use of barn-

the farmers in any other country
in the world (Figure 10).
d. Another factor
in the fertilizer program of Japan
is the contribution made by green-
manure crops to the fertility of
soils. Genge, soybeans, Medicago
Figure 10. - A compost neap
minima, common vetch, hairy vetch,
protected from the weather April.1946
and peas were the most widely used
green-manure crops in Japan from
1926 to 1945. The total average area devoted to green-manure crops each
year during this period was 466,663 hectares. These green-menure crops
produced an annual estimated return of 5,272,619 metric tons of green
manure to the soil, or an average of approximately 1l.3 metric tons to
each hectare on which they were grown (Tables 38 and 39). These green
manures contributed not only to the humus content of the soil, but the
following amounts of plant foods per hectare were also supplied; about
53.1 kilogrems of nitrogen, 12.4 kilograms of phosphoric acid, and 46.3
kilograms of potash.
e. Green manures are usually plented as a second crop,
often being seeded between the rows of rice, wheat, or barley before
harvest of the main crop. As a result of this cultural method, they
occupy only a small part of the area required for food crop production.
Green-manure crops are seldom sown in the small grain crop as is the
common practice in the United States. Neither is it a common practice
in Japan to grow green-manure crops in fields by themselves for green-
manuring purposes although this practice varies by agricultural dis-
tricts.
f. The farmers of Japan should increase the area in green-
manure crops, for they add organic matter to the soil as well as plant
nutrients. Green manures when incorporeted with the soil increase the
water-holding capacity of the soil, inprove soil structure and soil
εeration, decrease wind and water erosion, and stimulate other favorable
soil activity which promote increased plant growth and production of
crops. The other farm manures produce these desirable effects on the
soil to which they are applied, but probatly not to the same extent as
green manures. Organic commercial fertilizers add organic matter when
applied to the soil, but inorganic commercial fertilizers do not. This
is one reason why inorganic fertilizers are usually not as beneficial
to the soil as or anic fertilizers.
32

=== Page 67 === (confidence 0.99)
g. The fact that the usual crop rotation practices in Japan
consist of a rice-wheat, or rice-barley, or similar rotations of crops
without including a legume between the food-producing crops, increases
the need for fertilizers.
3. General
a. The soils of Japan are infertile because of their natural
composition and because they have been intensively cropped for many centu-
ries. It is necessary, therefore, that all availatle plant food elements
in the form of organic and inorganic (commercial and farm manures) ferti-
lizers be added to the soil every year to maintain the comparatively high
crop yields.
b. The need for fertilizers in Japan is verified by field
experiments with crops. Experimental data obtained from long-time field
experiments with fertilizers as applied to various crops growing under
different soil, climate and cultural conditions show conclusively the
real need of fertilizers for the production of crops in Japen. Results
obtained fror these field experiments indicete that elimination of nitro-
genous fertilizers considerably decreased the yields of rice, wheat, and
barley; ommission of phosphatic fertilizer materially reduced the yields
of wheat and barley, and nonapolication of potash decreased wheat, barley,
and sweetpotato yields.
c. Because of the normal expected increase in population
and the return of demobilized Jepanese military personnel and civilians
from abroad, additional food must be produced indigenously or be imported
from other countries.
d. Independent field spot checks from northern Hokkaido to
southern Kyushu, 25/ 26/ indicate that phosphorus is the most important
critical fertilizer element lacking in soils derived from volcanic ash.
Large areas of such soils are located in southern Hokkaido, the entire
Kanto Plain area, and southern Kyushu 27/. Field experiments with ferti-
lizers show that without phosphatic fertilizers, wheat and barley yields
drop more rapidly than rice yields.
e. It is believed that just as much food will be needed in
Japan in 1946 as in any previous year. It will be much cheaper, and
require less labor and ship tonnage space to import fertilizers for crop
25/ Report No 16, Natural Resources Section, pp 1-30, 26 January 1946
26
Report No 19 (A Preliminary Report), Natural Resources Section,
pp 1-19, 4 February 1946
271
Netural Resources of Japan, Natural Resources Section, pp 18-21, 22
May 1946
33

=== Page 68 === (confidence 0.98)
growing purposes than to import the foodstuffs which these fertilizers
coulå produce. Formerly, Japan produced about 80 to 85 percent of her
food requirements 28/. Because of the world food shortage, Japan should
produce maximum amounts of foodstuffe to meet her needs.
f. Priority of importation of raw materials or processed
fertilizers should be (1) phosphatic, (2) nitrogenous, and (3) potassic.
All of the phosphate rock recuired for producing conmercial phosphatic
fertilizers must be imported into Japan; 760.000 metric tons of com-
mercial nitroeenous fertilizers are expected to be produced indigenously
in 1946.
6. Priority of production 'of fertilizers in Japan should
he (1) nitrogenous and phosphatic; and (2) potassic fertilizers.
h. There is noreason why the maximun amounts of commercial
fertilizers requested by the Japanese Government (See paragraph H,2,b)
carnot be used advantageously for the growing of crops. Crop plants
require adequate and varied amounts of plant food for normal growth just
as people need the proper amounts and kinds of food for healthful living.
When e plent does not receive sufficient amounts of food at certain
critical stages of its life cycle, the growth of the plant is usually
stunted and it sometkmes fails to produce a crop. If production and
transportation facilities become better as time passes, it is believed
that additional amounts of commercial fertilizers up to the maximum
amounts requested by the Japanese Government should be made available to
the farmers of Japan. In this study, only the amounts of,commercial
fertilizers required for indigenous consumption are considered; it is
expected that none will be exported.

DEPARTMENT OF STATE
Memorandum of Conversation
DATE: December 12, 1946
SUBJECT:
PARTICIPANTS:
Mr. Martin - JK
Mr. Hodge - JK
Mr. Claxton - A-H
Mr. Myer - Standard Vacuum Oil Company
Mr. Seitz - Standard Vacuum Oil Company
COPIES TO:
894.659
746363
1-1493
On December 4, a conference was held between the State Department repre-
2-1246
sentatives listed above and representatives of the Standard Vacuum Oil Company
at the request of the latter on the question of U.S. policy with respect to
the level of petroleum refining to be permitted Japan.
iir. Myer stated that the Standard Vacuum Oil Company was disturbed by
newspaper reports to the effect that Ambassador Pauley had recommended re-
tention of 40,000 barrels per day refining capacity in Japan which he esti-
mated would mean approximately 12 million barrels per year. He seid that if
this were the U. S. policy he believed the effect would be the complete
elimination of Standard Vacuum's pre-war trade wit h Japan which consisted of
refined petroleum products and accounted for approximately 20% of all Japan's
CS/Y
pre-war refined petroleum imports. He stated that from information received
from the Petroleum Advisory Group attached to SCAP's staff the total Japanese
consumption of refined petroleum products in 1947 would be about 7 million
barrels; and would probably not exceed 10 or 12 million barrels before 1950.
He felt, therefore, the automatic effect of adopting Pauley's proposal would
be the complete substitution of crude to be refined by the Japanese for their
own use for that portion of refined products which they had imported before
the war.
574.659
Nr. Martin replied that there was as yet no U.S. policy with regard to
any specific level of refining capacity to remain in Japan and that in any
event U.S. policy when formulated would have to be negotiated with the other
FEC countries. He said that he wised to point out the fact that the U.S. was
committed to the general overall policy of cutting back productive capacity
5

=== Page 105 === (confidence 0.99)
in selected Japanese industries where security considerations were important,
and that the Japanese petroleum refining industry was a case in point. On
the other hand these cut-backs in industries which were only war supporting
and were not direct war industries, such as armament companies, could not be
so severe as to deprive the Japanese of a reasonable opportunity to meet their
minimum essential civilian requirements either through production for domestic
use or for exports to pay for necessary imports. In the case of petroleum re-
fining our decision to regard this as an industry subject to cut-back for reasons
of security was more of a protection to non-Japanese petroleum importers, such
as Standard Vacuum Oil Company, than  if we had decided to take no action
with respect to the level to be permitted the Japanese petroleum refining in-
dustry. The exact extent to which the Japanese petroleum refining industry
should be cut back for security purposes, consistent with leaving the Japanese
sufficient domestic economy was a problem. The Department was actively stu-
dying this problem and negotiating it within the U. S. Government. As yet,
no final decision has been made as to what this appropriate level should be
so far as U.S. policy is concerned.
Wr. Wartin further stated that our studies thus far had produced results
considerably at variance with the information received by the Standard Vacuum
Oil Company as to probable future Japanese requirements for petroleum products.
V.e estimated that in an early normal post war year the Japanese would require
nearly l8 million barrels annually of refined products; whereee, even assuming
the Pauley recommendation of 40,000 barrels per day to be adopted, this would
leave a margin between 4 to 6 million barrels for imports of refined products.
Moreover, even this assumption had to be modified in the light of currently
useable capacity in the Japanese refining industry which at present we under-
stood to be only 17,O00 barrels per day. Until Japanese industry had been
rehabilitated or additional units built to replace those badly damaged or
destroyed this would mean the Japanese would require 5 to 6 million additional
barrels per year which could be met only from imports of refined products.
The representatives of the tandard Vacuum Oil Company expressed some
doubts as to whether they would be able to maintain their pre-war imports of
refined products to Japan, either in whole or in part, even under these cir-
cumstances. These doubts were based on two considerations: (1) The Japanese
Government would doubtless try to discourage imports of refined products,
even though such imports were cheaper than Japanese refined products produced
from imported crudes, through the creation of preferential tarifs in favor
of crude as against refined imports; and (2) would encourage, through subsi-
dization or otherwise, the non-economic expansion of Japanese refining capacity
as they had done before the war.
After some discussion as to the source of crudes which Japan could rely
upon and the relative cost including transportation of refined versus crude,
Ir. artin stated that it was firm U.S. policy that Japan would be required
to extend most-favored nation treatment to all importers and that discrimina-
tory terrifs designed to favor one type of product over another would not be
permitted. Furthermore, it was U.S. policy that the Japanese would have to
extend to nationals of foreign countries the same rights and privileges which
it extended to its own nationals in maintaining or establishing industrial
enterprisesof whatever character in Japan. Therefore, as soon as private trade
with Japan could be allowed, Standard Vacuum Oil Company or any other foreign
oil company would, under this policy, be placed in a relatively more favorable
-2-

=== Page 106 === (confidence 0.97)
competitative position than it had occupied before the war to the extent
that it was actually able in a free competitive market to import refined
petroleum products at less cost than the Japanese could import crude and
refine it. Likewise, Standard Vacuum Oil Company or any other foreign
company could go into Japan and erect refineries under the same conditions
and with the same advantages as Japanese nationals could do so if they thought
this to be economically advantageous. In this connection kr. Martin pointed
out that after the initial cut-back of Japanese refining capacity to whatever
figure might be ultimately decided upon, the Japanese would be free, so far
as present U.S. policy thinking was concerned, either to rely upon imports of
finished petroleum products or to expand refining capacity in Japan,whichever
course was economically more feasible, provided that in neither case would
the Japanese be permitted to set up any artificial or discriminatory barriers
against nationals of other nations and in favor of their own nationals.
After some further discussion of estimated costs of rebuilding present
Japanese refining capacity and similar matters related to this problem, the
representatives of the Standard Vacuum Oil Company stated that they felt
somewhat, though not entirely, reassured; and requested that the statements
which they had made on behalf of the Standard Vacuum Oil Company be given care-
ful consideration. Mr. Martin again stated that the U.S. Government is still
considering the problem and that the statements made by the representatives
of the Standard Vacuum Oil Company would be carefully weighed together with
other evidence at our disposal in arriving at a decision as to the final level
of petroleum refining to be retained in Japan within the limits of the overall
general policy which he had previously outlined.
e化
JK:cLHodge
-3-

Honorable Charles E. Saltzman
Assistant Secretary of State
Dear Lr. Saltzman:
This is in answer to your conmunication dated 2 February 1948
introduced by mention of reported proposals for shipments of alumina
into Japan for processing there. As you doubtless know, reprèsenta-
tives of both the Reynolds Company and the Permanente Corporation,
subsequent to conferences vith representatives of the Departments of
State and Army, went to Japan to discuss with SCAP the possibility
of reducing alumina in Japanese plants. No proposals have yet been
received from SCAP as a result of these negotiations. In fact, SCAP
894.659
has made no proposals for importing alumina into Japan in order to
permit the export of aluminum ingot from Japan, or other proposals
for aluminum production for export which would necessarily require
approval by the United States Government.
As you know, a representative of the Permanente Corporation has
approached officials of this Department as well as the Department of
State concerning prospects for obtaining aluminum, aluminum products
and steel products from Japan. The Permanente representative sugges-
3-548
ted to Department of the Army representatives that his organization
and affiliates might be villing to supply raw materials and other im-
ports into Japan in order to accomplish the necessary production to
provide the products in which they were interested. It was suggested
to the Permanente representative that no policy difficulty appeared
to be involved in connection with their interest in obtaining steel
products. In connection with their interest in aluminum and aluminum
products, it was pointed out that negotiations in the FEC were some-
what delicate concerning approval for retention of aluminum producing
capacity in Japan. It was suggested that if they would emphasize their
interest in aluminun products, the matter might be handled more favor-
ably than would an interest in aluminum ingots. In any event, it was
suggested to them that any contracts which they might develop for ob-
taining either aluminum or aluminum products should guarantee that un-
der no circunstances vould the operation of the contract delay to any
significant degree reparations removals of aluminum prcducing capacity
once a policy determination is crystallized respecting the amount of
aluminun capacity which Japan may retain and the capacity which must
be made available for reparation renovals. The position expressed
above to the Permanente representative was discussed with .essrs. Dean
Bovaman and Burr Smith of O of your Department vho indicated general
agreement with the position as stated.

=== Page 116 === (confidence 0.96)
CONFIDENTIAL
We have as yet received no reports from SCAP concerning the
results of the discussions in Japan with Reynolds and Permanente
representatives. In addition, we have been withholding considera-
tion of and reply to SCAP's recent proposal for the production of
20,000 tons of aluminun ingots in 1948 against minimum yearly needs
of the Japanese economy of 15,000 metric tons of aluminum products.
The views of SCAP may have changed as a result of the Reynolds and
Permanente discussions. Consequently we are asking SCAP to report
on the status of his proposal and to co.ment on the discussions with
Reynolds and Permanente. When a reply has been received we may be
in a better position to discuss the points mentioned in your letter.
It is believed that SCAP is thoroughly acquainted with outstand-
ing directives and vith the terns of United States policy as adopted
by S..CC and SA ACC, as well as with the course of negotiations in
the Far astern Comnission as reflected in the various reports and
minutes of committee meetings published by the FeC. It would seem
unnecessary at this time, therefore, to send to SCAP the specific
terms of the reservations contained in your letter. In any case, it
would seem sufficient that arrangements made for production of alu-
minum or aluninum products for export assure that reparations removals
of aluminum capacity would not be unduly delayed by the operation of
such arrangements, and, of course, the Departments of State and Army
be kept informed of developments. If, however, you feel that SCAP is
not familiar with all directives and other pertinent materials pertain-
ing to the retention and operation of aluminum production capacity in
Japan so that additional information should be furnished to SCAP, the
Civil Affairs Division will collaborate with your representatives in
preparing a suitable communication to SCAP.
Wreen A haper
Sincerely yours,
William H. Drapen, Jr.

Tokyo, January 21, 1949.
No. 42
CONFIDENTIAL (FOR DEPARTMENT USE ONLY)
1
C
Subject: Military Vehicle Rebuilding Operation Under Supervision
( of United States Eighth Army in Japan.
OPY
RECEIVE
OEPARTET
Files
1979176
con-
OLL
Honorable
PHT(SO)
The Secretary of State,
Washington.
Sir:
As of present and future economic significance, I have the
honor to report the following observations made by Consul General
U. Alexis Johnson during a tour in this area of the "Big-5" opera-
tion of the United States Eighth Army, in company of the Eighth
Army Ordnance officer who has immediate responsibility for that
operation.
Because it was unable to obtain new vehicles from the United
States, and in view of the large stocks of spare parts available
in stockpiles accumulated during the war throughout the Pacific
2
islands, the Eighth Army has recently undertaken in Japan a major
vehicle rebuilding program. This program transcends the usual
4
x
basic maintenance operations of the Army, and is in itself a major
6
industrial undertaking patterned on commercial practices, entirely
811.211
different in approach from anything thus far undertaken by the
Occupation Forces in Japan. Because of its significance in train-
894.60
ing a considerable body of Japanese workmen and managers in the
most advanced techniques of American mass automotive production,
as well as other obvious economic considerations, it is reported
as being of interest to the Department.
The term "Big-5" represents "Base Industrial Group, Fifth
Echelon", which is a subsection of the Ordnance Section, Eighth
Army Headquarters, Yokohama. Except for the general supervision
exercised by the Ordnanee Officer, there are no military personnel
whatever in the operation which is staffed entirely by 19l Ameri-
cans who hold the status of Department of the Army civilian em-
ployees, and approximately 16,000 Japanese employees.
The project consists of four major operations -- (1) the Tokyo
Engineering Works for the rebuilding of Jeep and GMC "six by six"
truck engines and power trains; (2) the Opama Vehicle Rebuild Shops
located at the site of a former Japanese seaplane base between
Yokohama and Yokosuka; (3) the Akabane Tire Rebuild Plant in a form-
er Japanese Akabane Arsenal near Tokyo; and (4) the Sedan Rebuild
Plant
Bwh
CONFIDENTIAL
194

=== Page 125 === (confidence 0.99)
Tokyo's Despatch No. 42
of January 21, 1949.
-2
Plant at Nagoya.
The engine rebuild plant (1) is at present in full operation,
and is rebuilding approximately 3,000 engines a month. Machinery
for the power train portion of the plant is now being installed,
and the plant will be in operation within a few months.
The vehicle rebuild plant (2) is in course of having machinery
installed, and will have a designed capacity of 2,O00 jeeps and
"six by six" trucks a month, the operation being integrated with
that of the Tokyo Engineering Works.
The tire rebuild plant (3) will be in production shortly with
a designed capacity of 20,000 tires a month.
It is understood to be probable that the sedan rebuild plant
(4), with a designed capacity of 100 sedans a month, will not be in
operation until late in 1949.
Planning envisions having the entire operation in balanced
and capacity production in the early part of 1950.
Thus far, approximately 25,000 vehicles for rebuilding have
been received from other commands in the Pacific, in addition to
tens of thousands of vehicles in Japan requiring rebuilding. In
general, all of these vehicles are in a very deteriorated condi-
tion in consequence of prolonged misuse and exposure.
There have also been received annroximately 45,000 tons of
spare parts from various Pacific areas. While these parts have
also been subject to prolonged exposure, and the packing cases are
often almost entirely deteriorated, the contents are usually in ex-
cellent condition because protected by high grade moisture-proof
interior wrappings. As the case markings are often illegible, and
the parts were stored and shipped in great disorder, the task of
sorting and inventorying these shipments is stupendous.
The goal of the entire operation is to utilize all of these
parts and vehicles, supplemented by parts obtained from stockpiles
in the United States or manufactured by sub-contractors in Japan,
to place the maximum possible number of vehicles in virtually new
condition.
All materials, equipment and labor originating in Japan are
being obtained through the usual channels of procurement demands
on the Japanese Government. However, it is understood that the
SCAP foreign trade account will be credited with that portion in-
curred by the Japanese Government of the cost of rebuilding vehicles
furnished to units outside of Japan.
The motor rebuild plant visited at the Tokyo Engineering Works
is located in the former plant of the Mitsubishi Heavy Industries
Company tank factory in the southern section of Tokyo. The plant
was built immediately prior to the war, and consists of a series of
large and modern factory buildings covering approximately twenty-
five acres, well supplied with overhead electric cranes, a central
steam plant, and office space. As a commentary on Japanese effi-
ciency it is of interest to note that, in spite of the size and
modernity
CONFIDENTIAL

=== Page 126 === (confidence 0.99)
Tokyo's Despatch No. 42
of January 21, 1949.
-3-
modernity of the plant, as well as the large volume of heavy
materials handled, it was supplied entirely by truck over a single
narrow and unimproveddirt road. A small portion of the plant is
still being used by the company for the production of diesel truck
engines.
The motor rebuilding operation consists of two parallel con-
veyor lines, one for the four cylinder jeep engines, and the other
for the six cylinder GMC truck engines, tógether with all opera-
tions subsidiary thereto. As the engines are received they are
cleaned and completely disassembled, sub-assemblies such as electri-
cal, ignition and carburetor parts being removed for rebuilding in
the sub-assembly shops located between the two lines. The remainder
of the engine passes along the conveyor for reboring and honing of
the block, regrinding of the crankshaft, fitting of new pistons,
rings, bearings, and other necessary operations. As the engine pro-
gresses along the line, it is entirely rebuilt and sub-assemblies
are re-installed so that it emerges in virtually the same condition
as a new engine. Each engine is subjected to a rigorous two hours
and ten minutes dynamometer test after which it is painted and boxed
for shipment.
Most of the more complicated machinery such as crankshaft
grinders, cylinder reborers and dynamometers, are of American manu-
facture and have been supplied from Army stocks in the Pacific or
obtained from the United States. Labor and lobor management is
supplied by the Mitsubishi Heavy Industries Company at the rate of
¥9,000 (approximately $33.00 at the present military conversion
rate of ¥270 to U.S. $1.00) for each engine accepted. It is under-
stood that this present dollar eguivalent of the direct labor cost
is approximately the same as that for a similar operation in the
United States.
Some parts not obtainable by salvage or from stockpiles of new
parts are obtained from Japanese sub-contractors. Through close
supervision and inspection of the work of such contractors, the con-
cerned American personnel state that quality now approximates Ameri-
can standards. The volume of parts so procured is gradually being
increased.
To the untrained observer the plant gives an impression of
cleanliness, orderliness and smooth efficiency, entirely comparable
to any similar American plant. The American personnel appear to be
technically capable, and very enthusiastic in the approach to their
work.
It is obvious that with a few relative minor changes the plant
readily could be converted to the manufacture of new engines from
parts obtained from the growing number of sub-contractors engaged
in basic
CONFIDENTIAL

=== Page 127 === (confidence 0.97)
eA8 RAM
Tokyo's Despatch No. 42
of January 21, 1949.
-4
in basic manufacture. There also exists a nucleus of trained
American and Japanese personnel around which a considerable ex-
pansion of operations could rapidly be effected.
Respectfully yours,
W. J. Sebald
430.2
UAJohnson:hap

being directed.

Dear Mr. Dodge:
Thank you for sending me an informatiem sept,
forwarded by Colonel R. W. Porter of the Army Depart
ment, of Dr. Paul O'Leary's memorandum of Nareh 7 on
the "present Uneconomical Produotion of Soda Aah and
Caustio Soda".
The state of affairs described in this memoremde
seems to lie close to the heart of the Japanese stabi-
lization and recovery problems. Until wasteful, high
cost produotion methods resulting from spreading of
scarce materials among an excessive number of plants
for the sake of maintaining employment, preveating the
development of "excessive concentrations of economie
power" or for any other purpose has been stopped, pre-
duction of required greater quantities of materials at
prices the Japanese people can afford to pay and whieh
can be sold in foreign competition will not be mehieved.
At the same time we must realize, as I know you do,
that, although an essential part of the rationalisatien
process, dismissal of large numbers of workers who would
normally be retained under Japanese paternalistie empley
ment practices will present serious political as well
as economic problems if not dealt with through a greatly
expanded system of unemployment relief.
Your attack on the subsidy question, and the soon
to be established exchange rate for the yen should
greatly assist in rectifying the situation desoribed
C
În Dr. O'Leary'e memorandum. You may count on our
S
fullest support in carrying on the work you have begun.
I hope you will continue to forward us reports in
which you believe we would be interested. I am looking
forward
The Honorable
