14
Enclosure 'No. I to Despatch No. 260
dated April 27, 1949 from United States
Political Adviser for Japan, Tokyo, on
UNCLASSIFIED
the subject "TransmittingReport on
Japanese Chemical Plants Prepared by
Pope Chemical Mission to Japan."
PRESS RELEASE
General MacArthur today made public the Report entitled
"Japanese Chemical Plants" submitted by Mr. Frederick Pope to
the Supreme Commander on April 8th.
Mr. Pope, who was sent to Japan by the Secretary of Arimy to
survey the chemical industry, is former president of the Chemical
Construction Company of New York and was prominently identified
with the building of the Sumitomo ammonia plants in Japan between
1925 and 1932. He and members of his mission presently are en-
route to the United States where he will present his report to
Department of Army.
At the beginning of April 1949 the chemical industry pro-
duction index had reached 85.3% of the 1930-34 levels which have
been established by Far Eastern Commission as the standard for
a peacetime economy in Japan. At the end of 1945 the index stood
at 20.4% of the same base period. Within the past 44 months
significant strides have been registered in introducing into
Japan certain techniques and facilities for the commercial pro-
duction of important pharmaceuticals such as penicillin and of
improved insecticides, none of which were produced in the prewar
period. Significant progress also has been made in the mixing
of sub-standard fuels and petroleum coke in order to increase the
efficiency of the low grade coal mined in this country.
On April 14th the Holding Company Liquidation Commission re-
moved from designation as excessive concentrations of economic
power under provisions of Public Law No. 207, the Mitsui Chemical
Company, Nisshin Chemical Company, Mitsubishi Chemical Company and
Toyo High Pressure Company. Previously the Nissan Chemical Company
and Japan Soda Company had been similarly eliminated from pro-
visions of this law. One additional chemical company presently is
under consideration by the Deconcentration Review Board and a final
recommendation is expected in the near future.
The text of the report and recommendations follows:
1. The Under Secretary of the Army, with your acquies
cence I understand, invited me to report on the condition of
the Japanese chemical industry. To make such a report I have
visited, inspected, and questioned the management of forty-
two Japanese chemical plants. I beg to point out what is, of
course, self-evident, that it is impossible in a visit of a
day or part of a day to gain complete information about a
chemical plant. However, it is possible for a person with suf-
ficient background and experience to obtain educated impressions
which are, in general, correct. The conclusions I have reached
are clear to me.
2. The
UNCLASSIFIED

=== Page 141 === (confidence 0.99)
Enclosure No. 1 to
Tokyo's Despatch No. 260,
April 27, 1949.
- 2 -
2. The output of basic chemicals is increasing.
Curves showing the production of sulphuric acid, alkali,
ammonia, dyes, and other products are given in Appendix
A. The figures, in general, are given in Appendix B.
3. The cost of production as recorded for each plant
in Appendix C is a matter of concern. The general high
cost is due, I believe, in most cases to one or two of the
following: Poor management; labor costs and conditions;
partial operation of the plant because of shortages of
power, coal, or other raw materials; mechanical condition
of the plants; as well as government or war potential limi-
tation of production.
4. The first and most impertant deficiency is good
top men. It is particularly true in the chemical industry
that ood men at the top are sine qua non. The poorest sort
of a broken down plant with gocd management is far more likely
to succeed than the best up to date plant with incompetent
management. That may be called a platitude and true of all
industry, but it is peculiarly true of the chemical industry.
I feel the chemical companies should search the country for
good men no matter how cld they may be or what their present
circunstances are. Plant and company management, I find, is
frequently in the hands of ill-prepared men. On the other
hand, some companies are excellontly well managed.
5. The next need is money. Yow money may be had it is
not my province to say. I do believe, howeve, that, if a sub-
stantial sum of money, even many million yen, were loaned to
selected Japanese chemical companies at low interest - say 42% -
for terms varying from five to fifteen years, something between
95-986 (?) would be paid back.
6. fhe next need is raw material which includes fuel and
power. This is a  fficult problem, but I believe men and
money would solve it. Much sulphuric acid demand could be re-
lieved by usin other substances to combine with ammonia in
ammonia fertilizers. Another relief would be by using the sul-
phur dioxide in waste gases now thrown away as, for example, by
the Dowa Mining Company at Kosaka and the Nippon Mining Company
at Hitachi and Saganoseki. A further relief to the sulphur
situation would be the use of other carriers of nitrogen; for
example, as in the case of urea. It would pay to study the
action under Japanese conditions of ammonium chloride, a ferti-
lizer difficult to make and little used. Careful tests might
show the usability, under Japanese conditions, of nitrochalk
(a trade name for a mechanical mixture of ground limestone and
ammonium nitrate). I believe it would pay to make some nitro-
chalk without awaiting the tests. There are crops - say wheat,
for example - on which in most locations nitrochalk might be
used. It probably is necessary to obtain authority to manu-
facture the anmonium nitrate needed to produce nitrochalk.
The sulphur situation is of sufficient importance to warrant
the conclusion that Japan will probably be obliged to use sub-
stantial amounts of ammonium nitrate as such. The way it is
used
UNCLASSIFIED

=== Page 142 === (confidence 0.99)
Enclosure No. 1 to
Tokyo's Despatch No. 260,
April 27, 1949.
-3-
used, the form in which it may be used, and how the
granules may be covered should be studied earnestly and
expeditiously. This should not be done at the expense of
eny investigations concerning urea or ammonium chloride,
but concurrently, and the comparative costs and merits
determined.
7. There is no question but that salt is urgently
needed. It is brought now from long distances, most of it
coming from the Mediterranean and East African points.
Formerly e large amount cime from China and Manchuria. I
wonder if ne manufacture of salt in Japan should be en-
couraged, thus cutting down freight and the exporting of
money. he reestablishment of the industry in Manchuria
and other similar places should be encouraged as it would
cut down shipping costs, hence the cost of salt, and make
payment esier - possibly in goods.
8. he phosphate rock situation is bad. Phosphate
roek is being imported from the United States at great ex-
pense. If Japanese ships were available, and if the Japanese
were permitt A to do se he mining of phosphate rock in the
Pacific isl..is should be seriously considered.
9. Power in really not my subject. However, fluctuations
in hydroelectric cutput have serious effects on the operation
of chemical plants where continuous operation is the rule.
Therefore I fail to see why steam plants to even out the supply
in times of low water are not more common in Japan.
10. Fuel, too, is not my subject except as it affects
the chemical industry. I find that no coal benefaction other
then coal washing vith water is generally known in Japan.
Heavy Media Separation and "sink and float" processes would
eliminate up to three-quarters of the ash in most Japanese
coals. Were these installed at the mines, it would not only
greatly improve the quality of the coal, but also mean a
saving in freight of fifteen per cent, more or less. It is
very clear that, if not at the mines, they should be installed
in certain of the chemical plants to improve the usability of
the fuel.
11. I have not mentioned research which will be of the
utmost importance in the future. At the moment, however, I
feel that all Japanese efforts should be placed on the re-
habilitation of industry, letting commercial research follow
in a year or two.
12. Chemical patents are an essential and important
part of any chemical industry. I recall the situation in
Germany where the matter is being intensively studied. There
German citizens were, as late as last fall, permitted only
to file priority dates, which is entirely unsatisfactory to
them. I hope that not only will prompt study be given the
subject of Japanese patents but that Japanese chemical com-
panies will be permitted to purchase licenses to use American
patents.
13.As
UNCLASSIFIED

=== Page 143 === (confidence 0.99)
Enclosure No. l to
Tokyo's Despatch No. 260,
April 27, 1949.
-4-
13. As to reparations, the moving of chemical plants
for reparations can, I think, be explained only as revenge.
No chemical plant built by moving the machinery and apparatus
from another chemical plant can cost on the average less than
90-95% of the cost of a new plent and the result is a second-
hand plant - an abomination if not a liability to any chemical
manufacturer. I earnestly hope that this vicious procedure
may be stopped. I will mention four instances among many:
a. In one dye plant, the Nissin Chemical Company,
Limited, Osaka Plant, there is a plant producing sulphur tri-
oxide. It is, in effect, a part of a ten-ton sulphuric acid
plant. It is proposed to more it for reparations. It would
cost more to build a new plant by pulling that one down and
moving it then it would to build a new, complete sulphuric
acid plant, and yet, moving that plant, I am informed, would
throw two thousand men out of work.
b. nother case, the Asahi Chemical Company, Limited,
Nobecka Plant, away at the southem part of Kyushu, has a small
mercury cell caustic plant, part of a large chemical plant -
as a unit practically useless to anyone else. To move that for
reparations would, I am informed, throw eight hundred men out
of work. In that case the caustie and chlorine to be used in
the main plant must be brought in from plants lecated at a dis-
tince.
c. mother exmple that clearly stands out is the
Nippon Soda Company, Limited, Nihongi Plant. About ten percent
of the value of the plant has been marked for reparations. Be
that ten percent removed, it would, I em reliably advised, make
the vhole plant inoperative except as a loss, thus throwing
three thousend four hundred men cut of work. Everything they
make is fownded on caustic soda end chlorine. The plant was
started twenty years ago. The village and the surrounding vil-
lages ere largely dependent on its payroll for their existence.
The question immediately arises, why shouldn't the necessary
caustic soda and chlorine be imported from other plants? The
answer is that it would cost so much that the plant would soon
use up its capitel and would have to operate on a government
subsidy, or not at all. I believe the equipment it is proposed
to leave will produce an insufficient amount of chlorine and
caustic to permit the plant to operate at a profit. Here is a
clear case of, in effect, wiping out a community or paying a
government subsidy to meet a conclusion based on an individual
opinion.
d. The other side of the picture is illustrated at
the Nissan Chemical Industrial Company, Limited, Toyama Plant,
where there is a contact sulphuric acid plant that was moved
during the war by orders of the Japanese Army. I am told it
cost much more to move it than a new plant would have cost and
the plant is uns tisfactory. Of course, then they had an excuse -
time. It is, nevertheless, a good example of what should not be
done
UNCLASSIFIED

=== Page 144 === (confidence 0.97)
Enclosure No. 1 to
Tokyo's Despatch No. 260,
April 27, 1949.
-5-
done. Only thirty parcent f the original value could be
moved. Te cost of takin down, packing and transportation
wes much mere then thirty pereent when new.
In cll cases of electrolytic caustic soda plants
marked for reparations or as rplus, one must consider the
possi  lity of at some time using anmenium chloride as a
fertilizer, in which case all of the now surplus chlorine
would ie urgently needed.
Fur hermore, What a field for Communist agitation!
It is lmown to the workers at the plants mentioned in a, b,
and c khove that such action is contemplated. They are all
worried about their livel hood now and later and wondering if
their future: would net be more secure with the Communists
who pr mise them that, if Communisn comes in, the plant will
be ei her maintained as it is or restored. Also, uncertainty
as to the moving of a plant for reparetions is disconcerting
to menegement as well as to the workers.
14. Mhile I understand there are n. Fisoher-Tropsch or
similar plents operating todey in Japan, there are partially
di smounted plants. Polay there is no place for Fischer-
Tropsch pl nts in pn either from n economic or security
strndpoint. In one plaee a Fischer-Iropsch is housed in an
exc splion ly good bu lding that should be used for some other
purpose. Te process pperatus should he sold as sorap, sal-
vaging for cther purposes all standar machinery and equipment.
16. As  temporary measure, I recommend establishing an
Exesutive Engineering Soard in siap with an American as the
nominal heal with veto  were, ete., composed of twe first-
class Japanese chemical ceutives, two first-class Japanese
engineers, end tve firs -clees Americen chemical engineers
(men competent to earn in he neighborhood of $25,000 a year
in America). The object of this board would be to receive
each week the mos concise report of production, inventory,
shipment, and conditions of raw materials, labor and repairs
from, let us say, fifty to sixty Japanese chemical plants.
ith this information the Board should be able to help a
feltering apanese chemical company to its feet. Before the
war the Japanese chemical industry was a successful one. Its
routine operation needed no outside help. I feel such a board
as this would be unnecessary after say two years. In such
period of time it should be able to give the Japanese chemicel
industry the necessary help and encouragement.
17. The American euthorities must be urged to be very
sure of their facts before issuing drastic orders that may
have a long-time ffect on he Japanese chemical industry.
18. It
UNCLASSIFIED

=== Page 145 === (confidence 0.99)
Enclosure No. 1 to
Tckyo's Despatch No. 260,
April 27, 1949.
-6-
18. It is now impossible for most Japanese chemical
companies to get either foreign publications or foreign ad-
vice as they lack the necessary foreign exchange. Chemistry
has to go ahead or go backwards. There is no such thing as
standing still. This outside help and outside information
is & necessity.
19. I believe that Japanese salesmen should be per-
mitted to go abroad in the near future anticipating the ex-
port of such pharmaceuticals, dyes, and other chemical pro-
ducts as may soon be exported. To defer sending salesmen
abroad until the expo le products are on hand seems a poor
policy. Japan will have more than ordinary resistance to the
sele of her products due to the hate built up in various parts
of the world. Her highest possible caliber of men must pave
the way in many of the countries to which Japan would naturally
export.
20. The question of ships is very distinetly not part of
the chemical industry, but, on the other hand, transportation
of raw materiuls and finished products is an essential factor
in the chemical field. Some of the lowest class war-time mer-
chant ships laid up at home, if chartered to the Japanese and
run by Japanese crews, could make a lot of difference in the.
cost of importing salt and phosphate rock, moving coal between
the islands, etc.
21. The condition of Japanese chemical plants is not
very good, with two or three outstanding exceptions. We saw
one plant thet would be a model plant anywhere. We saw some
plants so bad as to be pathetic. The machine shops connected
with the various plants vary widely. Most of them are good.
Some of them have foundries. The Japanese mechanics, I have
observed over years, are, in general, very good. Lighting and
safety precautions are in most cases woefully pocr. Adequate
light should be installed. The same is true of safety pre-
cautions. Stair rails, belt guards, covering of holes in the
floor, etc., are necessary.
22. In practically all the Japanese chemical plants
there is a vast amount of rusty iron scrap; some neatly piled
up, some spread throughout the plant. This scrap has a very
real cash value which is lessening as the scrap market goes
down and the quality of this scrap deteriorates. The scrap is
frequently a very real detriment to the operation of the plants.
It is often in the way. It occupies space that could be used
for useful purposes and it has, without the slightest doubt, a
bad psychological effect on the workers. It is important that
workers should feel that they are working a well-managed,
business-like plant and to put them into a disorderly area is
wrong. I would like to see the chemical plants encouraged in
the removal of the scrap, possibly by a central purchasing
bureau. If possible, means to force the chemical plants to
clean up scrap should be used if they will not do it otherwise.
It
UNCLASSIFIED

=== Page 146 === (confidence 0.99)
Enclosure No. 1 to
Tokyo's Despatch No. 260,
April 27, 1949.
-7-
It might be necessary to have a traveling baling machine or
traveling shears, either of which could be mounted on a flat
car. I really know nothing about the scrap business.  These
remarks are from the point of view of a manufacturing chemist.
23. Something must be done about the allocations made
by the Japanese Government. There is, I believe, unmistakable
evidence that there is a great deal of favoritism and even dis-
honesty in he mabter of alloeations.
24. Communistic tendencies in the workmen are creating
an increasingly bad situation. In considering only the labor
in chemical plants, the advantages of something in the nature
of a community center occurred to me. This more properly
comes under the scope of Civil Education and Information, or
possibly Public Health and Welfare, than under the chemical
industry. However, I have made some observations which are
contained in Appendix D.
25. There is weakness in many of the new directorates
where unions have a hend in the election of directors. Some
theorists may favor it but it is wrong in theory. I am told
there are some cases where it hes been tried and abandcned.
Such directors are interested only in appeasing the unions.
Frequently directors now representing new shareholders have
no knowledge, interest or concern in the health of the in-
dustry, their only interest being what dividends may be had
for the s eholders they represent.
26. The policy of permitting corporations to pay the
salaries of union officials is manifestly end obviously wrong
and should b. forbidden.
27. As an additional conment on management, I wish to
point out that in the Arai Plant of the Dainippon Celluloid
Company, Limited, one of the best managed plants, the pro-
ductivity of labor is reported by the management to be four
percent above pre-war.
28. While I cannot be sure, I am strongly inclined to
believe that allocation is carried to a greater extent than
is necessary. On the other hand, a great deal of raw material
now allocated in the chemical industry should be free of al-
location. This would clean up some unfortunate situations and
be a step toward normalcy.
29. The manufacture of dyes in Japan, in general, is
well organized. The plants are reasonably good. The manage-
ment and technical men are better than average. If the dye
industry is allowed to proceed as it is going now, it should
make an early recovery and produce exportable goods. It would
be possible to go at length into this matter but all the in-
dustry really needs is an adequate supply of raw material and,
I repeat, to be let alone.

Enclosure No. 2 to Despatch No.
dated April 27, 1949 from United cates
Pelitical Adviser for Japan, Tokyo, on
UNCLASSIFIED
the subject "Transmitting Report on
Japanese Chemical Plants Prepared by
Pepe Chemical Mission to Japan."
(Copy of article put lished in
April 18, 194? issue of Mewsweek)
WHAT'S MRONG IN JAPAN? PLEIITY. BLASTS SURVEY
Japanese industrial produstion (it stands at only 60 per
cent of the 1930-34 level) lags behind that of every major power
wd th the possible exception of war-torn China. Japan is the
only country (again with the exception of China) not under the
direct control of Russia where Communism is not losing ground but
actually gaining.
In an effort to restore Japan': eonomy and reverse the trend
toward Conmmunism ashingten has reversed policles, sent missions.
of businessmen to Toy, an canducted deailed surveys of Japanese
industry. One of the mose searching surveys--of the Japanese chem-
ical industry--hes just been complated by Col. Frederick Pope, 71-
year-old forme. resident of the Chemical Construction Co. of New
York.
Pope gainel a worliwide r putation building 38 chemical plants
in 22 countries and served on the sar-productien board and in the
war mobilization set-up. He vecently surveyed the German chemical
industry and was peorsuaded t. l le sime jeb in Japan. His com-
pleted report was sulmitted to General MacArtbur April 2. Last week
Pope gave Mrs. helen J. Folater of Newsweck's Tokyo bureau a preview
of the report and his answers to the 64-yen yuestion: What's wrong
in Japan?
What's wrong in Japan, Pope felt, was pretty much what was
wrong in chemicals, since the difficulties in this fourth most im-
portant Japanese industry were basic and common to the entire economy.
He concluded that the chemical industry and the Japanese economy
needed two things--men andmoney.
Men: The much criticized "purge" of Japanese businessmen re-
moved at one stroke the best brains in Japanese industry. On this
point Pope commented:
"The purge has taken away the greatest asset the Japanese
chemical industry had. Wholesale reexamination is needed. Most of
the top men were honest, earnest, and hardworking. They did what
they were told. Most of them weren't interested in politics. These
purged men should be given the right to work to help take Japan off
the neck of the American taxpayer.
"Some of the new fellows in the chemical industry have done
very well considering their lack cf experience. Others are running
their
UNCLASSIFIED

=== Page 149 === (confidence 0.99)
Enclosure No. 2 to
Tokyo's Despatch No. 260,
April 27, 1949.
-2-
their plants into bankrupey, not purposely, they just don't know.
One important chemical plant is being run by a former bookkeeper."
Money: Pope thinks "the chemical industry has got to have
money. But the idea of giving money to Japan is no good. We
should Loan meney to Japanese industry through Japanese banks. I
would recem end an interest rate of around 42 per cent. Loans could
run from five to fifteen years. I say in my survey I believe 95 to
98 per cent of the loan would be repaid. I am convinced myself that
100 per cent would be repaid. 1f you lend to people instead of giv-
ing, they keep their self-respect and I think especially with the
Japanese this is true. The Japanese are long on self-respect.
"Along with the idea of loaning these Japanese companies
money, instead of iving it to them, I think it would be a good
idea to allow them to charter Anerican ships to transport salt, which
they now must import frem the Red Sea and the Mediterranean at ter-
rific transportation prices in American or British bottoms."
Spanking: Pope felt strongly about reparations: "In my opin-
ion, the only exeuse for reparations is revenge. As far as chemical
plants are concerned, reparations are an impossibility both in Ger-
many and in Japan. You can't move a chemical plant, tear it down,
and rebuild it as cheaply as you can build a new one. If you do
move one, you start with a socondhand plant-there's nothing worse
than a secondhand chemical plant. However, I've certainly recom-
mended razing to the ground the two Fischer Tropsch plants in Japan.
They are a war potential and show a high waste of raw materisla."
The ongineer questioned the practicility of plans by the Ameri-
can Deconcentration Review Board to break up big Japanese chemical
companies by removing plants or parts of plants: "The Deconcentration
Review Board should be spanked, sent home, and tcld to grow up. It
is high time someone realizel that only a real expert in each field
is equipped to deal practically with such problems. If decentraliza-
tion is to be considered at all, it should be done wisely and con-
servatively. I also recommend that production limitations be re-
viewed."
Figures: Pope went on: "I have seen an example of the mis-
takes that can be made here by an untrained, inexperienced man who,
unfortunately, is considered an expert by the occupation. Not only
is this man just a 'lad,: lut after talking with him, I brought out
the fact that all he really knew about the chemical business was what
he had picked up here in Japan and from a few books he had read before.
However, it rested upon this so-called expert to make a very import-
ant deci sion about the Japanese chemical industry.
"There are three operating alkali plants in Japan, one running
at 50 per cent production, the other two at 30 per cent. It was de-
cided to close down two of these plants for one year. I don't know,
myself

Subject: Information on Japanese Woven Wire Industry.
INFOR
FR
DCR
FE
The Chargé d'Affaires ad interim has the honor to refer to this
OLI
Mission's despatch no. 370 of June 10, 1949, entitled "Information
894.659/6-2049
COM
on Production of Ferroalloys in Japan," and to transmit herewith in-
TAR
formation on the Japanese woven wire industry supplied under date of
FRB
June 13, 1949, by the Industry Division, Economic and Scientific
Section (ESS), General Headquarters, SCAP, to the United Kingdom
Liaison Mission in Japan. The latter requested the information under
date of May 24, 1949, on behalf of the Board of Trade, London.
I SIISI
There are at present 79 factories engaged in the woven wire in-
dustry in Japan. The total number of machines in wire netting and
fence manufacturing is 2,325. Their present approximate proportions
JUL61949
PA  SAT
of use are 57 per cent for woven wire cloth and 34 per cent for chain
link fencing. The balance of the machines is used for producing
other types of netting and fencing.
The available production figures quoted by the ESS Industry Divi-
sicn give output of woven wire cloth at 570 and 2.322 metric tons re-
spectively in 194? and 1948; output of chain link fencing during the
same two years is reported at 1, 193 and 2, 461 metric tons respectively.
No record is available of prewar exports; exports of woven wire cloth
in 1947 aggregated 190 metric tons at $55, 200, with exports in 1948
increasing to 420 metric tons at $126, 000. No exports of chain link
fencing were made in 1947 and 1948.
The current number of operatives in the Japanese woven wire in-
dustry is 3,400. The 79 factories engaged in this industry are members
of three associations, including the Kansai Kanaami Konwakai (Kansai
Wire Netting Association) with 48 members; the Kantc Kanaami Konwakai
(Kanto Wire Netting Association) with 28 members; and the Shindo
Kogyokai (Shindo Industrial Association) with 3 menbers.
In response to a previous inquiry from the United Kingdom Liaison
Mission in Japan, the ESs Industry Division under date of January 31,
1949, stated that 82 factories were then engaged in the woven wire in-
dustry, with total number of looms being 1, 832 and number of operatives
being 3,333.
It is believed that information of the foregoing type on lesser
known industries of Japan may prove useful to the Department and to
other United States Government agencies.

2. As far as is known, picric acid in commercial quantities is
not being manufactured in Japan. Chloropicrin is being manufactured
from surplus stocks of picric acid. Mitsui ChemicalIndustrial Co.
states that picric acid was manufactured at their plant by nitration
of phenol at approximately the temperature given in the refereneed
memorandin. While 18-8 stainless steel has been used in the past,.
the company considers this material ratherluxurious for the quality
of picric acid required as an intermediate inthe manufacture of
chloropicrin. Normally, porcelain kettles are employed. It is re-
ported however, that 18-8 stainless steel does not inhibit the
nitration
3. Hit sbishi ChenicalIndustrial Company formerly manufactured
picric aoid from mono-chlorbenzene which was ritrated in a mild steel
kettle with cast iron cooling pipes to dinitro-benzene, starting under
45℃ and reaching l05℃ maximwn. After saponification to dinitro-
chlorphenol, the final nitration to picric aci: was accomplishedin an
18-8 stainless steel water-jacketed kettlestarting below 36℃ and
reaching 95℃ maximum.
L. Nisshin Chemical Company and Nippon Aayaku Company formerly
manufactured picric acid by processes similar to that used by the
Mitsubishi Company. Nisshin employed a react: on kettle of chrome nickel
steel, the composition of which has not been : earned. Nippon Kayaku
Company employed cast iron ruaction vessels. The latter companyhad
attempted to use porcelain vessels but has enecountered considerable
difficulty due to cracks developing during the reaction.

Tokyo's Despatch No. 899,
December 23, 1949.
Ir. Class lists the folloving "bright spots on the horizon"
which assertedly lend credence to his optimistic forecast: (1)
completion of noteworthy foreign trade arrangements and improved
export prospects; (2) increased availability of raw materials
from logical sources of supply at reduced costs; (3) a more ade-
quate supply of indigenous materials, notably coal; (4) an en-
larged merchant marine; (5) improved efficieney of operations
resulting from advice nd reconmendations of visiting experts from
the United States; (é) rationalipation within industry; (7) avail-
alility of U.S. Aid Coanterpart Funds for industrial rehabilitation;
(a) increased represertation abroad; (9) a balanced budget and more
stable economy; (lo) rolaxed measures for import-export transantions,
and (1l) prospects for an early peace. treaty.
In closing his statement, I. Class points out that "concerted
efforts and continued teamwork on the part of all concerned cannot
fail to make 1950 a banner year and an important milestone on the
read of industrial recovery for Japan."
